The Tragedy of Romeo and Juliet

William Shakespeare — EMOTHE0153_TheTragedyOfRomeoAndJuliet

Act

The Prologue

⸢Enter⸣ Chorus.

CHORUS

1Two households, both alike in dignity
2(In fair Verona, where we lay our scene),
3From ancient grudge break to new mutiny,
4Where civil blood makes civil hands unclean.
5From forth the fatal loins of these two foes
6A pair of star-crossed lovers take their life;
7Whose misadventured piteous overthrows
8Doth with their death bury their parent's strife.
9The fearful passage of their death-marked love
10And the continuance of their parent's rage,
11Which, but their children's end, naught could remove,
12Is now the two hours' traffic of our stage;
13The which, if you with patient ears attend,
14What here shall miss, our toil shall strive to mend.

⸢Chorus exits.⸣

Act I

⸢Scene 1⸣

Enter Sampson and Gregory, with swords and bucklers, of the house of Capulet.

SAMPSON

Gregory, on my word we’ll not carry coals.

GREGORY

No, for then we should be colliers.

SAMPSON

I mean, an we be in choler, we’ll draw.

GREGORY

Ay, while you live, draw your neck out of
collar.

SAMPSON

I strike quickly, being moved.

GREGORY

But thou art not quickly moved to strike.

SAMPSON

A dog of the house of Montague moves me.

GREGORY

To move is to stir, and to be valiant is to
stand. Therefore if thou art moved thou runn’st
away.

SAMPSON

A dog of that house shall move me to stand. I
will take the wall of any man or maid of Montague’s.

GREGORY

That shows thee a weak slave, for the weakest
goes to the wall.

SAMPSON

’Tis true, and therefore women, being the
weaker vessels, are ever thrust to the wall. Therefore
I will push Montague’s men from the wall and
thrust his maids to the wall.

GREGORY

The quarrel is between our masters and us
their men.

SAMPSON

’Tis all one. I will show myself a tyrant.
When I have fought with the men, I will be civil
with the maids; I will cut off their heads.

GREGORY

The heads of the maids?

SAMPSON

Ay, the heads of the maids, or their maidenheads.
Take it in what sense thou wilt.

GREGORY

They must take it ⸢in⸣ sense that feel it.

SAMPSON

Me they shall feel while I am able to stand,
and ’tis known I am a pretty piece of flesh.

GREGORY

’Tis well thou art not fish; if thou hadst, thou
hadst been poor-john. Draw thy tool. Here comes
of the house of Montagues.

Enter ⸢Abram with another Servingman⸣.

SAMPSON

My naked weapon is out. Quarrel, I will back
thee.

GREGORY

How? Turn thy back and run?

SAMPSON

Fear me not.

GREGORY

No, marry. I fear thee!

SAMPSON

Let us take the law of our sides; let them
begin.

GREGORY

I will frown as I pass by, and let them take it
as they list.

SAMPSON

Nay, as they dare. I will bite my thumb at
them, which is disgrace to them if they bear it.

⸢He bites his thumb⸣.

ABRAM

Do you bite your thumb at us, sir?

SAMPSON

I do bite my thumb, sir.

ABRAM

Do you bite your thumb at us, sir?

SAMPSON,

⸢aside to Gregory⸣ Is the law of our side if I
say "Ay"?

GREGORY,

⸢aside to Sampson⸣ No.

SAMPSON

No, sir, I do not bite my thumb at you, sir,
but I bite my thumb, sir.

GREGORY

Do you quarrel, sir?

ABRAM

Quarrel, sir? No, sir.

SAMPSON

But if you do, sir, I am for you. I serve as
good a man as you.

ABRAM

No better.

SAMPSON

Well, sir.

Enter Benvolio.

GREGORY,

⸢aside to Sampson⸣ Say "better"; here comes
one of my master’s kinsmen.

SAMPSON

Yes, better, sir.

ABRAM

You lie.

SAMPSON

Draw if you be men.—Gregory, remember
thy washing blow.

They fight.

BENVOLIO

Part, fools! ⸢Drawing his sword⸣. Put up your swords. You know not what you do.

Enter Tybalt, ⸢drawing his sword⸣.

TYBALT

15What, art thou drawn among these heartless hinds?
16Turn thee, Benvolio; look upon thy death.

BENVOLIO

17I do but keep the peace. Put up thy sword,
18Or manage it to part these men with me.

TYBALT

19What, drawn and talk of peace? I hate the word
20As I hate hell, all Montagues, and thee.
21Have at thee, coward!

⸢They fight⸣.

Enter three or four Citizens with clubs or partisans.

⸢CITIZENS⸣

22Clubs, bills, and partisans! Strike! Beat them down!
23Down with the Capulets! Down with the Montagues!

Enter old Capulet in his gown, and his Wife.

CAPULET

24What noise is this? Give me my long sword, ho!

LADY CAPULET

25A crutch, a crutch! Why call you for a sword?

Enter old Montague and his Wife.

CAPULET

26My sword, I say. Old Montague is come
27And flourishes his blade in spite of me.

MONTAGUE

28Thou villain Capulet!—Hold me not; let me go.

LADY MONTAGUE

29Thou shalt not stir one foot to seek a foe.

Enter Prince Escalus with his train.

PRINCE

30Rebellious subjects, enemies to peace,
31Profaners of this neighbor-stainèd steel—
32Will they not hear?—What ho! You men, you beasts,
33That quench the fire of your pernicious rage
34With purple fountains issuing from your veins:
35On pain of torture, from those bloody hands
36Throw your mistempered weapons to the ground,
37And hear the sentence of your movèd prince.
38Three civil brawls bred of an airy word
39By thee, old Capulet, and Montague,
40Have thrice disturbed the quiet of our streets
41And made Verona’s ancient citizens
42Cast by their grave-beseeming ornaments
43To wield old partisans in hands as old,
44Cankered with peace, to part your cankered hate.
45If ever you disturb our streets again,
46Your lives shall pay the forfeit of the peace.
47For this time all the rest depart away.
48You, Capulet, shall go along with me,
49And, Montague, come you this afternoon
50To know our farther pleasure in this case,
51To old Free-town, our common judgment-place.
52Once more, on pain of death, all men depart.

⸢All but Montague, Lady Montague, and Benvolio⸣ exit.

MONTAGUE

53, ⸢to Benvolio⸣ Who set this ancient quarrel new abroach?
54Speak, nephew, were you by when it began?

BENVOLIO

55Here were the servants of your adversary,
56And yours, close fighting ere I did approach.
57I drew to part them. In the instant came
58The fiery Tybalt with his sword prepared,
59Which, as he breathed defiance to my ears,
60He swung about his head and cut the winds,
61Who, nothing hurt withal, hissed him in scorn.
62While we were interchanging thrusts and blows
63Came more and more and fought on part and part,
64Till the Prince came, who parted either part.

LADY MONTAGUE

65O, where is Romeo? Saw you him today?
66Right glad I am he was not at this fray.

BENVOLIO

67Madam, an hour before the worshiped sun
68Peered forth the golden window of the east,
69A troubled mind ⸢drove⸣ me to walk abroad,
70Where underneath the grove of sycamore
71That westward rooteth from this city side,
72So early walking did I see your son.
73Towards him I made, but he was ’ware of me
74And stole into the covert of the wood.
75I, measuring his affections by my own
76(Which then most sought where most might not be found,
77Being one too many by my weary self),
78Pursued my humor, not pursuing his,
79And gladly shunned who gladly fled from me.

MONTAGUE

80Many a morning hath he there been seen,
81With tears augmenting the fresh morning’s dew,
82Adding to clouds more clouds with his deep sighs.
83But all so soon as the all-cheering sun
84Should in the farthest east begin to draw
85The shady curtains from Aurora’s bed,
86Away from light steals home my heavy son
87And private in his chamber pens himself,
88Shuts up his windows, locks fair daylight out,
89And makes himself an artificial night.
90Black and portentous must this humor prove,
91Unless good counsel may the cause remove.

BENVOLIO

92My noble uncle, do you know the cause?

MONTAGUE

93I neither know it nor can learn of him.

BENVOLIO

94Have you importuned him by any means?

MONTAGUE

95Both by myself and many other friends.
96But he, ⸢his⸣ own affections’ counselor,
97Is to himself—I will not say how true,
98But to himself so secret and so close,
99So far from sounding and discovery,
100As is the bud bit with an envious worm
101Ere he can spread his sweet leaves to the air
102Or dedicate his beauty to the same.
103Could we but learn from whence his sorrows grow,
104We would as willingly give cure as know.

Enter Romeo.

BENVOLIO

105See where he comes. So please you, step aside.
106I’ll know his grievance or be much denied.

MONTAGUE

107I would thou wert so happy by thy stay
108To hear true shrift.—Come, madam, let’s away.

⸢Montague and Lady Montague⸣ exit.

BENVOLIO

109Good morrow, cousin.

ROMEO

Good morrow, cousin. Is the day so young?

BENVOLIO

110But new struck nine.

ROMEO

But new struck nine. Ay me, sad hours seem long.
111Was that my father that went hence so fast?

BENVOLIO

112It was. What sadness lengthens Romeo’s hours?

ROMEO

113Not having that which, having, makes them short.

BENVOLIO

In love?

ROMEO

Out—

BENVOLIO

Of love?

ROMEO

114Out of her favor where I am in love.

BENVOLIO

115Alas that love, so gentle in his view,
116Should be so tyrannous and rough in proof!

ROMEO

117Alas that love, whose view is muffled still,
118Should without eyes see pathways to his will!
119Where shall we dine?—O me! What fray was here?
120Yet tell me not, for I have heard it all.
121Here’s much to do with hate, but more with love.
122Why then, O brawling love, O loving hate,
123O anything of nothing first ⸢create!⸣
124O heavy lightness, serious vanity,
125Misshapen chaos of ⸢well-seeming⸣ forms,
126Feather of lead, bright smoke, cold fire, sick health,
127Still-waking sleep that is not what it is!
128This love feel I, that feel no love in this.
129Dost thou not laugh?

BENVOLIO

Dost thou not laugh? No, coz, I rather weep.

ROMEO

130Good heart, at what?

BENVOLIO

Good heart, at what? At thy good heart’s oppression.

ROMEO

131Why, such is love’s transgression.
132Griefs of mine own lie heavy in my breast,
133Which thou wilt propagate to have it pressed
134With more of thine. This love that thou hast shown
135Doth add more grief to too much of mine own.
136Love is a smoke made with the fume of sighs;
137Being purged, a fire sparkling in lovers’ eyes;
138Being vexed, a sea nourished with loving tears.
139What is it else? A madness most discreet,
140A choking gall, and a preserving sweet.
141Farewell, my coz.

BENVOLIO

Farewell, my coz. Soft, I will go along.
142An if you leave me so, you do me wrong.

ROMEO

143Tut, I have lost myself. I am not here.
144This is not Romeo. He’s some other where.

BENVOLIO

145Tell me in sadness, who is that you love?

ROMEO

146What, shall I groan and tell thee?

BENVOLIO

147Groan? Why, no. But sadly tell me who.

ROMEO

148A sick man in sadness makes his will—
149A word ill urged to one that is so ill.
150In sadness, cousin, I do love a woman.

BENVOLIO

151I aimed so near when I supposed you loved.

ROMEO

152A right good markman! And she’s fair I love.

BENVOLIO

153A right fair mark, fair coz, is soonest hit.

ROMEO

154Well in that hit you miss. She’ll not be hit
155With Cupid’s arrow. She hath Dian’s wit,
156And, in strong proof of chastity well armed,
157From love’s weak childish bow she lives uncharmed.
158She will not stay the siege of loving terms,
159Nor bide th’ encounter of assailing eyes,
160Nor ope her lap to saint-seducing gold.
161O, she is rich in beauty, only poor
162That, when she dies, with beauty dies her store.

BENVOLIO

163Then she hath sworn that she will still live chaste?

ROMEO

164She hath, and in that sparing ⸢makes⸣ huge waste;
165For beauty, starved with her severity,
166Cuts beauty off from all posterity.
167She is too fair, too wise, wisely too fair,
168To merit bliss by making me despair.
169She hath forsworn to love, and in that vow
170Do I live dead, that live to tell it now.

BENVOLIO

171Be ruled by me. Forget to think of her.

ROMEO

172O, teach me how I should forget to think!

BENVOLIO

173By giving liberty unto thine eyes.
174Examine other beauties.

ROMEO

Examine other beauties. ’Tis the way
175To call hers, exquisite, in question more.
176These happy masks that kiss fair ladies’ brows,
177Being black, puts us in mind they hide the fair.
178He that is strucken blind cannot forget
179The precious treasure of his eyesight lost.
180Show me a mistress that is passing fair;
181What doth her beauty serve but as a note
182Where I may read who passed that passing fair?
183Farewell. Thou canst not teach me to forget.

BENVOLIO

184I’ll pay that doctrine or else die in debt.

They exit.

⸢Scene 2⸣

Enter Capulet, County Paris, and ⸢a Servingman.⸣

CAPULET

185But Montague is bound as well as I,
186In penalty alike, and ’tis not hard, I think,
187For men so old as we to keep the peace.

PARIS

188Of honorable reckoning are you both,
189And pity ’tis you lived at odds so long.
190But now, my lord, what say you to my suit?

CAPULET

191But saying o’er what I have said before.
192My child is yet a stranger in the world.
193She hath not seen the change of fourteen years.
194Let two more summers wither in their pride
195Ere we may think her ripe to be a bride.

PARIS

196Younger than she are happy mothers made.

CAPULET

197And too soon marred are those so early made.
198Earth hath swallowed all my hopes but she;
199She’s the hopeful lady of my earth.
200But woo her, gentle Paris, get her heart;
201My will to her consent is but a part.
202And, she agreed, within her scope of choice
203Lies my consent and fair according voice.
204This night I hold an old accustomed feast,
205Whereto I have invited many a guest
206Such as I love; and you among the store,
207One more, most welcome, makes my number more.
208At my poor house look to behold this night
209Earth-treading stars that make dark heaven light.
210Such comfort as do lusty young men feel
211When well-appareled April on the heel
212Of limping winter treads, even such delight
213Among fresh fennel buds shall you this night
214Inherit at my house. Hear all, all see,
215And like her most whose merit most shall be;
216Which, on more view of many, mine, being one,
217May stand in number, though in reck’ning none.
218Come go with me.
Come go with me. ⸢To Servingman, giving him a list⸣. Go, sirrah, trudge about
219Through fair Verona, find those persons out
220Whose names are written there, and to them say
221My house and welcome on their pleasure stay.

⸢Capulet and Paris⸣ exit.

SERVINGMAN

Find them out whose names are written
here! It is written that the shoemaker should
meddle with his yard and the tailor with his last, the
fisher with his pencil and the painter with his nets.
But I am sent to find those persons whose names
are here writ, and can never find what names the
writing person hath here writ. I must to the learned.
In good time!

Enter Benvolio and Romeo.

BENVOLIO

222, ⸢to Romeo⸣ Tut, man, one fire burns out another’s burning;
223One pain is lessened by another’s anguish.
224Turn giddy, and be helped by backward turning.
225One desperate grief cures with another’s languish.
226Take thou some new infection to thy eye,
227And the rank poison of the old will die.

ROMEO

228Your plantain leaf is excellent for that.

BENVOLIO

229For what, I pray thee?

ROMEO

For what, I pray thee? For your broken shin.

BENVOLIO

230Why Romeo, art thou mad?

ROMEO

231Not mad, but bound more than a madman is,
232Shut up in prison, kept without my food,
233Whipped and tormented, and—good e’en, good fellow.

SERVINGMAN

God gi’ good e’en. I pray, sir, can you
read?

ROMEO

234Ay, mine own fortune in my misery.

SERVINGMAN

Perhaps you have learned it without
book. But I pray, can you read anything you see?

ROMEO

235Ay, if I know the letters and the language.

SERVINGMAN

You say honestly. Rest you merry.

ROMEO

236Stay, fellow. I can read.

(He reads the letter.)

237Signior Martino and his wife and daughters,
238County Anselme and his beauteous sisters,
239The lady widow of Vitruvio,
240Signior Placentio and his lovely nieces,
241Mercutio and his brother Valentine,
242Mine Uncle Capulet, his wife and daughters,
243My fair niece Rosaline and Livia,
244Signior Valentio and his cousin Tybalt,
245Lucio and the lively Helena.
246A fair assembly. Whither should they come?

SERVINGMAN

Up.

ROMEO

Whither? To supper?

SERVINGMAN

To our house.

ROMEO

Whose house?

SERVINGMAN

My master’s.

ROMEO

247Indeed I should have asked thee that before.

SERVINGMAN

Now I’ll tell you without asking. My
master is the great rich Capulet, and, if you be not
of the house of Montagues, I pray come and crush a
cup of wine. Rest you merry.

⸢He exits.⸣

BENVOLIO

248At this same ancient feast of Capulet’s
249Sups the fair Rosaline whom thou so loves,
250With all the admirèd beauties of Verona.
251Go thither, and with unattainted eye
252Compare her face with some that I shall show,
253And I will make thee think thy swan a crow.

ROMEO

254When the devout religion of mine eye
255Maintains such falsehood, then turn tears to fire;
256And these who, often drowned, could never die,
257Transparent heretics, be burnt for liars.
258One fairer than my love? The all-seeing sun
259Ne’er saw her match since first the world begun.

BENVOLIO

260Tut, you saw her fair, none else being by,
261Herself poised with herself in either eye;
262But in that crystal scales let there be weighed
263Your lady’s love against some other maid
264That I will show you shining at this feast,
265And she shall scant show well that now seems best.

ROMEO

266I’ll go along, no such sight to be shown,
267But to rejoice in splendor of mine own.

⸢They exit.⸣

⸢Scene 3⸣

Enter ⸢Lady Capulet⸣ and Nurse.

LADY CAPULET

268Nurse, where’s my daughter? Call her forth to me.

NURSE

269Now, by my maidenhead at twelve year old,
270I bade her come.—What, lamb! What, ladybird!
271God forbid. Where’s this girl? What, Juliet!

Enter Juliet.

JULIET

How now, who calls?

NURSE

Your mother.

JULIET

272Madam, I am here. What is your will?

LADY CAPULET

273This is the matter.—Nurse, give leave awhile.
274We must talk in secret.—Nurse, come back again.
275I have remembered me, thou ’s hear our counsel.
276Thou knowest my daughter’s of a pretty age.

NURSE

277Faith, I can tell her age unto ⸢an⸣ hour.

LADY CAPULET

She’s not fourteen.

NURSE

I’ll lay fourteen of my teeth (and yet, to my teen
be it spoken, I have but four) she’s not fourteen.
How long is it now to Lammastide?

LADY CAPULET

A fortnight and odd days.

NURSE

278Even or odd, of all days in the year,
279Come Lammas Eve at night shall she be fourteen.
280Susan and she (God rest all Christian souls!)
281Were of an age. Well, Susan is with God;
282She was too good for me. But, as I said,
283On Lammas Eve at night shall she be fourteen.
284That shall she. Marry, I remember it well.
285’Tis since the earthquake now eleven years,
286And she was weaned (I never shall forget it)
287Of all the days of the year, upon that day.
288For I had then laid wormwood to my dug,
289Sitting in the sun under the dovehouse wall.
290My lord and you were then at Mantua.
291Nay, I do bear a brain. But, as I said,
292When it did taste the wormwood on the nipple
293Of my dug and felt it bitter, pretty fool,
294To see it tetchy and fall out with ⸢the⸣ dug.
295"Shake," quoth the dovehouse. ’Twas no need, I trow,
296To bid me trudge.
297And since that time it is eleven years.
298For then she could stand high-lone. Nay, by th’rood,
299She could have run and waddled all about,
300For even the day before, she broke her brow,
301And then my husband (God be with his soul,
302He was a merry man) took up the child.
303"Yea," quoth he, "Dost thou fall upon thy face?
304Thou wilt fall backward when thou hast more wit,
305Wilt thou not, Jule?" And, by my holidam,
306The pretty wretch left crying and said "Ay."
307To see now how a jest shall come about!
308I warrant, an I should live a thousand years,
309I never should forget it. "Wilt thou not, Jule?" quoth he.
310And, pretty fool, it stinted and said "Ay."

LADY CAPULET

311Enough of this. I pray thee, hold thy peace.

NURSE

312Yes, madam, yet I cannot choose but laugh
313To think it should leave crying and say "Ay."
314And yet, I warrant, it had upon its brow
315A bump as big as a young cock’rel’s stone,
316A perilous knock, and it cried bitterly.
317"Yea," quoth my husband. "Fall’st upon thy face?
318Thou wilt fall backward when thou comest to age,
319Wilt thou not, Jule?" It stinted and said "Ay."

JULIET

320And stint thou, too, I pray thee, nurse, say I.

NURSE

321Peace. I have done. God mark thee to his grace,
322Thou wast the prettiest babe that e’er I nursed.
323An I might live to see thee married once,
324I have my wish.

LADY CAPULET

325Marry, that "marry" is the very theme
326I came to talk of.—Tell me, daughter Juliet,
327How stands your ⸢disposition⸣ to be married?

JULIET

328It is an ⸢honor⸣ that I dream not of.

NURSE

329An ⸢honor⸣? Were not I thine only nurse,
330I would say thou hadst sucked wisdom from thy teat.

LADY CAPULET

331Well, think of marriage now. Younger than you
332Here in Verona, ladies of esteem,
333Are made already mothers. By my count
334I was your mother much upon these years
335That you are now a maid. Thus, then, in brief:
336The valiant Paris seeks you for his love.

NURSE

337A man, young lady—lady, such a man
338As all the world—why, he’s a man of wax.

LADY CAPULET

339Verona’s summer hath not such a flower.

NURSE

340Nay, he’s a flower, in faith, a very flower.

LADY CAPULET

341What say you? Can you love the gentleman?
342This night you shall behold him at our feast.
343Read o’er the volume of young Paris’ face,
344And find delight writ there with beauty’s pen.
345Examine every married lineament
346And see how one another lends content,
347And what obscured in this fair volume lies
348Find written in the margent of his eyes.
349This precious book of love, this unbound lover,
350To beautify him only lacks a cover.
351The fish lives in the sea, and ’tis much pride
352For fair without the fair within to hide.
353That book in many’s eyes doth share the glory
354That in gold clasps locks in the golden story.
355So shall you share all that he doth possess
356By having him, making yourself no less.

NURSE

357No less? Nay, bigger. Women grow by men.

LADY CAPULET

358Speak briefly. Can you like of Paris’ love?

JULIET

359I’ll look to like, if looking liking move.
360But no more deep will I endart mine eye
361Than your consent gives strength to make ⸢it⸣ fly.

Enter ⸢Servingman.⸣

SERVINGMAN

Madam, the guests are come, supper
served up, you called, my young lady asked for, the
Nurse cursed in the pantry, and everything in
extremity. I must hence to wait. I beseech you,
follow straight.

LADY CAPULET

362We follow thee.
We follow thee. ⸢Servingman exits.⸣ Juliet, the County stays.

⸢Scene 4⸣

Enter Romeo, Mercutio, Benvolio, with five or six other Maskers, Torchbearers, ⸢and a Boy with a drum.⸣

ROMEO

364What, shall this speech be spoke for our excuse?
365Or shall we on without apology?

BENVOLIO

366The date is out of such prolixity.
367We’ll have no Cupid hoodwinked with a scarf,
368Bearing a Tartar’s painted bow of lath,
369Scaring the ladies like a crowkeeper,
370⸢Nor no without-book prologue, faintly spoke
371After the prompter, for our entrance⸣.
372But let them measure us by what they will.
373We’ll measure them a measure and be gone.

ROMEO

374Give me a torch. I am not for this ambling.
375Being but heavy I will bear the light.

MERCUTIO

376Nay, gentle Romeo, we must have you dance.

ROMEO

377Not I, believe me. You have dancing shoes
378With nimble soles. I have a soul of lead
379So stakes me to the ground I cannot move.

MERCUTIO

380You are a lover. Borrow Cupid’s wings
381And soar with them above a common bound.

ROMEO

382I am too sore enpiercèd with his shaft
383To soar with his light feathers, and so bound
384I cannot bound a pitch above dull woe.
385Under love’s heavy burden do I sink.

⸢MERCUTIO⸣

386And to sink in it should you burden love—
387Too great oppression for a tender thing.

ROMEO

388Is love a tender thing? It is too rough,
389Too rude, too boist’rous, and it pricks like thorn.

MERCUTIO

390If love be rough with you, be rough with love.
391Prick love for pricking, and you beat love down.—
392Give me a case to put my visage in.—
393A visor for a visor. What care I
394What curious eye doth cote deformities?
395Here are the beetle brows shall blush for me.

BENVOLIO

396Come, knock and enter, and no sooner in
397But every man betake him to his legs.

ROMEO

398A torch for me. Let wantons light of heart
399Tickle the senseless rushes with their heels,
400For I am proverbed with a grandsire phrase:
401I’ll be a candle holder and look on;
402The game was ne’er so fair, and I am ⸢done.⸣

MERCUTIO

403Tut, dun’s the mouse, the constable’s own word.
404If thou art dun, we’ll draw thee from the mire—
405Or, save ⸢your⸣ reverence, love—wherein thou stickest
406Up to the ears. Come, we burn daylight, ho!

ROMEO

407Nay, that’s not so.

MERCUTIO

Nay, that’s not so. I mean, sir, in delay
408We waste our lights; in vain, ⸢light⸣ lights by day.
409Take our good meaning, for our judgment sits
410Five times in that ere once in our ⸢five⸣ wits.

ROMEO

411And we mean well in going to this masque,
412But ’tis no wit to go.

MERCUTIO

But ’tis no wit to go. Why, may one ask?

ROMEO

413I dreamt a dream tonight.

MERCUTIO

I dreamt a dream tonight. And so did I.

ROMEO

414Well, what was yours?

MERCUTIO

Well, what was yours? That dreamers often lie.

ROMEO

415In bed asleep while they do dream things true.

MERCUTIO

416O, then I see Queen Mab hath been with you.
417She is the fairies’ midwife, and she comes
418In shape no bigger than an agate stone
419On the forefinger of an alderman,
420Drawn with a team of little ⸢atomi⸣
421Over men’s noses as they lie asleep.
422Her wagon spokes made of long spinners’ legs,
423The cover of the wings of grasshoppers,
424Her traces of the smallest spider web,
425Her collars of the moonshine’s wat’ry beams,
426Her whip of cricket’s bone, the lash of film,
427Her wagoner a small gray-coated gnat,
428Not half so big as a round little worm
429Pricked from the lazy finger of a ⸢maid.⸣
430Her chariot is an empty hazelnut,
431Made by the joiner squirrel or old grub,
432Time out o’ mind the fairies’ coachmakers.
433And in this state she gallops night by night
434Through lovers’ brains, and then they dream of love;
435On courtiers’ knees, that dream on cur’sies straight;
436O’er lawyers’ fingers, who straight dream on fees;
437O’er ladies’ lips, who straight on kisses dream,
438Which oft the angry Mab with blisters plagues
439Because their ⸢breaths⸣ with sweetmeats tainted are.
440Sometime she gallops o’er a courtier’s nose,
441And then dreams he of smelling out a suit.
442And sometime comes she with a tithe-pig’s tail,
443Tickling a parson’s nose as he lies asleep;
444Then he dreams of another benefice.
445Sometime she driveth o’er a soldier’s neck,
446And then dreams he of cutting foreign throats,
447Of breaches, ambuscadoes, Spanish blades,
448Of healths five fathom deep, and then anon
449Drums in his ear, at which he starts and wakes
450And, being thus frighted, swears a prayer or two
451And sleeps again. This is that very Mab
452That plats the manes of horses in the night
453And bakes the ⸢elflocks⸣ in foul sluttish hairs,
454Which once untangled much misfortune bodes.
455This is the hag, when maids lie on their backs,
456That presses them and learns them first to bear,
457Making them women of good carriage.
458This is she—

ROMEO

This is she— Peace, peace, Mercutio, peace.
459Thou talk’st of nothing.

MERCUTIO

Thou talk’st of nothing. True, I talk of dreams,
460Which are the children of an idle brain,
461Begot of nothing but vain fantasy,
462Which is as thin of substance as the air
463And more inconstant than the wind, who woos
464Even now the frozen bosom of the north
465And, being angered, puffs away from thence,
466Turning his side to the dew-dropping south.

BENVOLIO

467This wind you talk of blows us from ourselves.
468Supper is done, and we shall come too late.

ROMEO

469I fear too early, for my mind misgives
470Some consequence yet hanging in the stars
471Shall bitterly begin his fearful date
472With this night’s revels, and expire the term
473Of a despisèd life closed in my breast
474By some vile forfeit of untimely death.
475But he that hath the steerage of my course
476Direct my ⸢sail.⸣ On, lusty gentlemen.

BENVOLIO

Strike, drum.

They march about the stage and ⸢then withdraw to the side.⸣

502, ⸢to a Servingman⸣ What lady’s that which doth enrich the hand

TYBALT

514This, by his voice, should be a Montague.—
515Fetch me my rapier, boy.
Fetch me my rapier, boy. ⸢Page exits.⸣ What, dares the slave

540You’ll make a mutiny among my guests,

TYBALT

CAPULET

544This trick may chance to scathe you. I know what.
545You must contrary me. Marry, ’tis time—
546Well said, my hearts.—You are a princox, go.

548I’ll make you quiet.—What, cheerly, my hearts!

TYBALT

549Patience perforce with willful choler meeting
550Makes my flesh tremble in their different greeting.
551I will withdraw, but this intrusion shall,
552Now seeming sweet, convert to bitt’rest gall.

He exits.

ROMEO

553, ⸢taking Juliet’s hand⸣ If I profane with my unworthiest hand
554This holy shrine, the gentle sin is this:
555My lips, two blushing pilgrims, ready stand
556To smooth that rough touch with a tender kiss.

JULIET

557Good pilgrim, you do wrong your hand too much,
558Which mannerly devotion shows in this;
559For saints have hands that pilgrims’ hands do touch,
560And palm to palm is holy palmers’ kiss.

ROMEO

561Have not saints lips, and holy palmers too?

JULIET

562Ay, pilgrim, lips that they must use in prayer.

ROMEO

563O then, dear saint, let lips do what hands do.
564They pray: grant thou, lest faith turn to despair.

JULIET

565Saints do not move, though grant for prayers’ sake.

ROMEO

566Then move not while my prayer’s effect I take.

⸢He kisses her.⸣

567Thus from my lips, by thine, my sin is purged.

JULIET

568Then have my lips the sin that they have took.

ROMEO

570Give me my sin again.

⸢He kisses her⸣.

JULIET

Act II

⸢Enter⸣ Chorus.

CHORUS

604Now old desire doth in his deadbeth lie,
605And young affection gapes to be his heir.
606That fair for which love groaned for and would die,
607With tender Juliet ⸢matched⸣, is now not fair.
608Now Romeo is beloved and loves again,
609Alike bewitchèd by the charm of looks,
610But to his foe supposed he must complain,
611And she steal love’s sweet bait from fearful hooks.
612Being held a foe, he may not have access
613To breathe such vows as lovers use to swear,
614And she as much in love, her means much less
615To meet her new belovèd anywhere.
616But passion lends them power, time means, to meet,
617Temp’ring extremities with extreme sweet.

⸢Chorus exits.⸣

⸢Scene 1⸣

Enter Romeo alone.

ROMEO

618Can I go forward when my heart is here?
619Turn back, dull earth, and find thy center out.

⸢He withdraws.⸣

Enter Benvolio with Mercutio.

BENVOLIO

620Romeo, my cousin Romeo, Romeo!

MERCUTIO

621He is wise
622And, on my life, hath stol’n him home to bed.

BENVOLIO

623He ran this way and leapt this orchard wall.
624Call, good Mercutio.

⸢MERCUTIO⸣

Call, good Mercutio. Nay, I’ll conjure too.
625Romeo! Humors! Madman! Passion! Lover!
626Appear thou in the likeness of a sigh.
627Speak but one rhyme and I am satisfied.
628Cry but "Ay me," ⸢pronounce⸣ but "love" and ⸢"dove."⸣
629Speak to my gossip Venus one fair word,
630One nickname for her purblind son and ⸢heir,⸣
631Young Abraham Cupid, he that shot so ⸢trim⸣
632When King Cophetua loved the beggar maid.—
633He heareth not, he stirreth not, he moveth not.
634The ape is dead, and I must conjure him.—
635I conjure thee by Rosaline’s bright eyes,
636By her high forehead, and her scarlet lip,
637By her fine foot, straight leg, and quivering thigh,
638And the demesnes that there adjacent lie,
639That in thy likeness thou appear to us.

BENVOLIO

640An if he hear thee, thou wilt anger him.

MERCUTIO

641This cannot anger him. ’Twould anger him
642To raise a spirit in his mistress’ circle
643Of some strange nature, letting it there stand
644Till she had laid it and conjured it down.
645That were some spite. My invocation
646Is fair and honest. In his mistress’ name,
647I conjure only but to raise up him.

BENVOLIO

648Come, he hath hid himself among these trees
649To be consorted with the humorous night.
650Blind is his love and best befits the dark.

MERCUTIO

651If love be blind, love cannot hit the mark.
652Now will he sit under a medlar tree
653And wish his mistress were that kind of fruit
654As maids call medlars when they laugh alone.—
655O Romeo, that she were, O, that she were
656An ⸢open-arse,⸣ thou a pop’rin pear.
657Romeo, good night. I’ll to my truckle bed;
658This field-bed is too cold for me to sleep.—
659Come, shall we go?

BENVOLIO

Come, shall we go? Go, then, for ’tis in vain
660To seek him here that means not to be found.

⸢They⸣ exit.

⸢Scene 2⸣

⸢Romeo comes forward.⸣

ROMEO

661He jests at scars that never felt a wound.

⸢Enter Juliet above.⸣

662But soft, what light through yonder window breaks?
663It is the East, and Juliet is the sun.
664Arise, fair sun, and kill the envious moon,
665Who is already sick and pale with grief
666That thou, her maid, art far more fair than she.
667Be not her maid since she is envious.
668Her vestal livery is but sick and green,
669And none but fools do wear it. Cast it off.
670It is my lady. O, it is my love!
671O, that she knew she were!
672She speaks, yet she says nothing. What of that?
673Her eye discourses; I will answer it.
674I am too bold. ’Tis not to me she speaks.
675Two of the fairest stars in all the heaven,
676Having some business, ⸢do⸣ entreat her eyes
677To twinkle in their spheres till they return.
678What if her eyes were there, they in her head?
679The brightness of her cheek would shame those stars
680As daylight doth a lamp; her eye in heaven
681Would through the airy region stream so bright
682That birds would sing and think it were not night.
683See how she leans her cheek upon her hand.
684O, that I were a glove upon that hand,
685That I might touch that cheek!

JULIET

That I might touch that cheek! Ay me.

ROMEO

That I might touch that cheek! Ay me. , ⸢aside⸣ She speaks.aparte
686O, speak again, bright angel, for thou art
687As glorious to this night, being o’er my head,
688As is a wingèd messenger of heaven
689Unto the white-upturnèd wond’ring eyes
690Of mortals that fall back to gaze on him
691When he bestrides the lazy puffing clouds
692And sails upon the bosom of the air.

JULIET

693O Romeo, Romeo, wherefore art thou Romeo?
694Deny thy father and refuse thy name,
695Or, if thou wilt not, be but sworn my love,
696And I’ll no longer be a Capulet.

ROMEO

697, ⸢aside⸣ Shall I hear more, or shall I speak at this?aparte

JULIET

698’Tis but thy name that is my enemy.
699Thou art thyself, though not a Montague.
700What’s Montague? It is nor hand, nor foot,
701Nor arm, nor face. O, be some other name
702Belonging to a man.
703What’s in a name? That which we call a rose
704By any other word would smell as sweet.
705So Romeo would, were he not Romeo called,
706Retain that dear perfection which he owes
707Without that title. Romeo, doff thy name,
708And, for thy name, which is no part of thee,
709Take all myself.

ROMEO

Take all myself. I take thee at thy word.
710Call me but love, and I’ll be new baptized.
711Henceforth I never will be Romeo.

JULIET

712What man art thou that, thus bescreened in night,
713So stumblest on my counsel?

ROMEO

So stumblest on my counsel? By a name
714I know not how to tell thee who I am.
715My name, dear saint, is hateful to myself
716Because it is an enemy to thee.
717Had I it written, I would tear the word.

JULIET

718My ears have yet not drunk a hundred words
719Of thy tongue’s uttering, yet I know the sound.
720Art thou not Romeo, and a Montague?

ROMEO

721Neither, fair maid, if either thee dislike.

JULIET

722How camest thou hither, tell me, and wherefore?
723The orchard walls are high and hard to climb,
724And the place death, considering who thou art,
725If any of my kinsmen find thee here.

ROMEO

726With love’s light wings did I o’erperch these walls,
727For stony limits cannot hold love out,
728And what love can do, that dares love attempt.
729Therefore thy kinsmen are no stop to me.

JULIET

730If they do see thee, they will murder thee.

ROMEO

731Alack, there lies more peril in thine eye
732Than twenty of their swords. Look thou but sweet,
733And I am proof against their enmity.

JULIET

734I would not for the world they saw thee here.

ROMEO

735I have night’s cloak to hide me from their eyes,
736And, but thou love me, let them find me here.
737My life were better ended by their hate
738Than death proroguèd, wanting of thy love.

JULIET

739By whose direction found’st thou out this place?

ROMEO

740By love, that first did prompt me to inquire.
741He lent me counsel, and I lent him eyes.
742I am no pilot; yet, wert thou as far
743As that vast shore ⸢washed⸣ with the farthest sea,
744I should adventure for such merchandise.

JULIET

745Thou knowest the mask of night is on my face,
746Else would a maiden blush bepaint my cheek
747For that which thou hast heard me speak tonight.
748Fain would I dwell on form; fain, fain deny
749What I have spoke. But farewell compliment.
750Dost thou love me? I know thou wilt say "Ay,"
751And I will take thy word. Yet, if thou swear’st,
752Thou mayst prove false. At lovers’ perjuries,
753They say, Jove laughs. O gentle Romeo,
754If thou dost love, pronounce it faithfully.
755Or, if thou thinkest I am too quickly won,
756I’ll frown and be perverse and say thee nay,
757So thou wilt woo, but else not for the world.
758In truth, fair Montague, I am too fond,
759And therefore thou mayst think my ⸢havior⸣ light.
760But trust me, gentleman, I’ll prove more true
761Than those that have ⸢more⸣ coying to be strange.
762I should have been more strange, I must confess,
763But that thou overheard’st ere I was ware
764My true-love passion. Therefore pardon me,
765And not impute this yielding to light love,
766Which the dark night hath so discoverèd.

ROMEO

767Lady, by yonder blessèd moon I vow,
768That tips with silver all these fruit-tree tops—

JULIET

769O, swear not by the moon, th’ inconstant moon,
770That monthly changes in her ⸢circled⸣ orb,
771Lest that thy love prove likewise variable.

ROMEO

772What shall I swear by?

JULIET

What shall I swear by? Do not swear at all.
773Or, if thou wilt, swear by thy gracious self,
774Which is the god of my idolatry,
775And I’ll believe thee.

ROMEO

And I’ll believe thee. If my heart’s dear love—

JULIET

776Well, do not swear. Although I joy in thee,
777I have no joy of this contract tonight.
778It is too rash, too unadvised, too sudden,
779Too like the lightning, which doth cease to be
780Ere one can say "It lightens." Sweet, good night.
781This bud of love, by summer’s ripening breath,
782May prove a beauteous flower when next we meet.
783Good night, good night. As sweet repose and rest
784Come to thy heart as that within my breast.

ROMEO

785O, wilt thou leave me so unsatisfied?

JULIET

786What satisfaction canst thou have tonight?

ROMEO

787Th’ exchange of thy love’s faithful vow for mine.

JULIET

788I gave thee mine before thou didst request it,
789And yet I would it were to give again.

ROMEO

790Wouldst thou withdraw it? For what purpose, love?

JULIET

791But to be frank and give it thee again.
792And yet I wish but for the thing I have.
793My bounty is as boundless as the sea,
794My love as deep. The more I give to thee,
795The more I have, for both are infinite.

⸢Nurse calls from within⸣.

796I hear some noise within. Dear love, adieu.—
797Anon, good nurse.—Sweet Montague, be true.
798Stay but a little; I will come again.

⸢She exits⸣.

ROMEO

799O blessèd, blessèd night! I am afeard,
800Being in night, all this is but a dream,
801Too flattering sweet to be substantial.

⸢Reenter Juliet above⸣.

JULIET

802Three words, dear Romeo, and good night indeed.
803If that thy bent of love be honorable,
804Thy purpose marriage, send me word tomorrow,
805By one that I’ll procure to come to thee,
806Where and what time thou wilt perform the rite,
807And all my fortunes at thy foot I’ll lay
808And follow thee my ⸢lord⸣ throughout the world.

⸢NURSE

, within⸣ Madam.

JULIET

809I come anon.—But if thou meanest not well,
810I do beseech thee—

⸢NURSE

, within⸣ Madam.

JULIET

811By and by, I come.—
812To cease thy strife and leave me to my grief.
813Tomorrow will I send.

ROMEO

814So thrive my soul—

JULIET

815A thousand times good night.

⸢She exits.⸣

ROMEO

816A thousand times the worse to want thy light.
817Love goes toward love as schoolboys from their books,
818But love from love, toward school with heavy looks.

⸢Going.⸣

Enter Juliet ⸢above⸣ again.

JULIET

819Hist, Romeo, hist! O, for a falc’ner’s voice
820To lure this tassel-gentle back again!
821Bondage is hoarse and may not speak aloud,
822Else would I tear the cave where Echo lies
823And make her airy tongue more hoarse than ⸢mine⸣
824With repetition of "My Romeo!"

ROMEO

825It is my soul that calls upon my name.
826How silver-sweet sound lovers’ tongues by night,
827Like softest music to attending ears.

JULIET

828Romeo.

ROMEO

Romeo. My ⸢dear.⸣

JULIET

Romeo. My ⸢dear.⸣ What o’clock tomorrow
829Shall I send to thee?

ROMEO

Shall I send to thee? By the hour of nine.

JULIET

830I will not fail. ’Tis twenty year till then.
831I have forgot why I did call thee back.

ROMEO

832Let me stand here till thou remember it.

JULIET

833I shall forget, to have thee still stand there,
834Rememb’ring how I love thy company.

ROMEO

835And I’ll still stay, to have thee still forget,
836Forgetting any other home but this.

JULIET

837’Tis almost morning. I would have thee gone,
838And yet no farther than a wanton’s bird,
839That lets it hop a little from his hand,
840Like a poor prisoner in his twisted gyves,
841And with a silken thread plucks it back again,
842So loving-jealous of his liberty.

ROMEO

843I would I were thy bird.

JULIET

I would I were thy bird. Sweet, so would I.
844Yet I should kill thee with much cherishing.
845Good night, good night. Parting is such sweet sorrow
846That I shall say "Good night" till it be morrow.

⸢She exits.⸣

⸢ROMEO⸣

847Sleep dwell upon thine eyes, peace in thy breast.
848Would I were sleep and peace so sweet to rest.
849Hence will I to my ghostly friar’s close cell,
850His help to crave, and my dear hap to tell.

He exits.

⸢Scene 3⸣

Enter Friar ⸢Lawrence⸣ alone with a basket.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

851The gray-eyed morn smiles on the frowning night,
852⸢Check’ring⸣ the eastern clouds with streaks of light,
853And fleckled darkness like a drunkard reels
854From forth day’s path and Titan’s ⸢fiery⸣ wheels.
855Now, ere the sun advance his burning eye,
856The day to cheer and night’s dank dew to dry,
857I must upfill this osier cage of ours
858With baleful weeds and precious-juicèd flowers.
859The Earth that’s nature’s mother is her tomb;
860What is her burying grave, that is her womb;
861And from her womb children of divers kind
862We sucking on her natural bosom find,
863Many for many virtues excellent,
864None but for some, and yet all different.
865O, mickle is the powerful grace that lies
866In plants, herbs, stones, and their true qualities.
867For naught so vile that on the Earth doth live
868But to the Earth some special good doth give;
869Nor aught so good but, strained from that fair use,
870Revolts from true birth, stumbling on abuse.
871Virtue itself turns vice, being misapplied,
872And vice sometime by action dignified.

Enter Romeo.

873Within the infant rind of this weak flower
874Poison hath residence and medicine power:
875For this, being smelt, with that part cheers each part;
876Being tasted, stays all senses with the heart.
877Two such opposèd kings encamp them still
878In man as well as herbs—grace and rude will;
879And where the worser is predominant,
880Full soon the canker death eats up that plant.

ROMEO

881Good morrow, father.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

Good morrow, father. Benedicite.
882What early tongue so sweet saluteth me?
883Young son, it argues a distempered head
884So soon to bid "Good morrow" to thy bed.
885Care keeps his watch in every old man’s eye,
886And, where care lodges, sleep will never lie;
887But where unbruisèd youth with unstuffed brain
888Doth couch his limbs, there golden sleep doth reign.
889Therefore thy earliness doth me assure
890Thou art uproused with some distemp’rature,
891Or, if not so, then here I hit it right:
892Our Romeo hath not been in bed tonight.

ROMEO

893That last is true. The sweeter rest was mine.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

894God pardon sin! Wast thou with Rosaline?

ROMEO

895With Rosaline, my ghostly father? No.
896I have forgot that name and that name’s woe.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

897That’s my good son. But where hast thou been then?

ROMEO

898I’ll tell thee ere thou ask it me again.
899I have been feasting with mine enemy,
900Where on a sudden one hath wounded me
901That’s by me wounded. Both our remedies
902Within thy help and holy physic lies.
903I bear no hatred, blessèd man, for, lo,
904My intercession likewise steads my foe.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

905Be plain, good son, and homely in thy drift.
906Riddling confession finds but riddling shrift.

ROMEO

907Then plainly know my heart’s dear love is set
908On the fair daughter of rich Capulet.
909As mine on hers, so hers is set on mine,
910And all combined, save what thou must combine
911By holy marriage. When and where and how
912We met, we wooed, and made exchange of vow
913I’ll tell thee as we pass, but this I pray,
914That thou consent to marry us today.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

915Holy Saint Francis, what a change is here!
916Is Rosaline, that thou didst love so dear,
917So soon forsaken? Young men’s love then lies
918Not truly in their hearts, but in their eyes.
919Jesu Maria, what a deal of brine
920Hath washed thy sallow cheeks for Rosaline!
921How much salt water thrown away in waste
922To season love, that of it doth not taste!
923The sun not yet thy sighs from heaven clears,
924Thy old groans yet ringing in mine ancient ears.
925Lo, here upon thy cheek the stain doth sit
926Of an old tear that is not washed off yet.
927If e’er thou wast thyself, and these woes thine,
928Thou and these woes were all for Rosaline.
929And art thou changed? Pronounce this sentence then:
930Women may fall when there’s no strength in men.

ROMEO

931Thou chid’st me oft for loving Rosaline.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

932For doting, not for loving, pupil mine.

ROMEO

933And bad’st me bury love.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

And bad’st me bury love. Not in a grave
934To lay one in, another out to have.

ROMEO

935I pray thee, chide me not. Her I love now
936Doth grace for grace and love for love allow.
937The other did not so.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

The other did not so. O, she knew well
938Thy love did read by rote, that could not spell.
939But come, young waverer, come, go with me.
940In one respect I’ll thy assistant be,
941For this alliance may so happy prove
942To turn your households’ rancor to pure love.

ROMEO

943O, let us hence. I stand on sudden haste.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

944Wisely and slow. They stumble that run fast.

They exit.

⸢Scene 4⸣

Enter Benvolio and Mercutio.

MERCUTIO

945Where the devil should this Romeo be?
946Came he not home tonight?

BENVOLIO

947Not to his father’s. I spoke with his man.

MERCUTIO

948Why, that same pale hard-hearted wench, that Rosaline,
949Torments him so that he will sure run mad.

BENVOLIO

950Tybalt, the kinsman to old Capulet,
951Hath sent a letter to his father’s house.

MERCUTIO

A challenge, on my life.

BENVOLIO

Romeo will answer it.

MERCUTIO

Any man that can write may answer a letter.

BENVOLIO

Nay, he will answer the letter’s master, how
he dares, being dared.

MERCUTIO

Alas, poor Romeo, he is already dead,
stabbed with a white wench’s black eye, run
through the ear with a love-song, the very pin of his
heart cleft with the blind bow-boy’s butt shaft. And
is he a man to encounter Tybalt?

⸢BENVOLIO⸣

Why, what is Tybalt?

MERCUTIO

More than prince of cats. O, he’s the courageous
captain of compliments. He fights as you sing
prick-song, keeps time, distance, and proportion.
He rests his minim rests, one, two, and the third in
your bosom—the very butcher of a silk button, a
duelist, a duelist, a gentleman of the very first house
of the first and second cause. Ah, the immortal
passado, the punto reverso, the hay !

BENVOLIO

The what?

MERCUTIO

The pox of such antic, lisping, affecting
⸢phantasimes,⸣ these new tuners of accent: "By
Jesu, a very good blade! A very tall man! A very good
whore!" Why, is not this a lamentable thing, grandsire,
that we should be thus afflicted with these
strange flies, these fashion-mongers, these ⸢pardon-me ’s⸣,
who stand so much on the new form
that they cannot sit at ease on the old bench? O their
bones, their bones!

Enter Romeo.

BENVOLIO

Here comes Romeo, here comes Romeo.

MERCUTIO

Without his roe, like a dried herring. O
flesh, flesh, how art thou fishified! Now is he for the
numbers that Petrarch flowed in. Laura to his lady
was a kitchen wench (marry, she had a better love
to berhyme her), Dido a dowdy, Cleopatra a gypsy,
Helen and Hero hildings and harlots, Thisbe a gray
eye or so, but not to the purpose.—Signior Romeo,
bonjour. There’s a French salutation to your French
slop. You gave us the counterfeit fairly last night.

ROMEO

Good morrow to you both. What counterfeit
did I give you?

MERCUTIO

The slip, sir, the slip. Can you not conceive?

ROMEO

Pardon, good Mercutio, my business was
great, and in such a case as mine a man may strain
courtesy.

MERCUTIO

That’s as much as to say such a case as
yours constrains a man to bow in the hams.

ROMEO

Meaning, to curtsy.

MERCUTIO

Thou hast most kindly hit it.

ROMEO

A most courteous exposition.

MERCUTIO

Nay, I am the very pink of courtesy.

ROMEO

"Pink" for flower.

MERCUTIO

Right.

ROMEO

Why, then is my pump well flowered.

MERCUTIO

Sure wit, follow me this jest now till thou
hast worn out thy pump, that when the single sole
of it is worn, the jest may remain, after the wearing,
solely singular.

ROMEO

O single-soled jest, solely singular for the
singleness.

MERCUTIO

Come between us, good Benvolio. My wits
faints.

ROMEO

Switch and spurs, switch and spurs, or I’ll cry
a match.

MERCUTIO

Nay, if our wits run the wild-goose chase, I
am done, for thou hast more of the wild goose in
one of thy wits than, I am sure, I have in my whole
five. Was I with you there for the goose?

ROMEO

Thou wast never with me for anything when
thou wast not there for the goose.

MERCUTIO

I will bite thee by the ear for that jest.

ROMEO

Nay, good goose, bite not.

MERCUTIO

Thy wit is a very bitter sweeting; it is a most
sharp sauce.

ROMEO

And is it not, then, well served into a sweet
goose?

MERCUTIO

O, here’s a wit of cheveril that stretches
from an inch narrow to an ell broad.

ROMEO

I stretch it out for that word "broad," which
added to the goose, proves thee far and wide a
broad goose.

MERCUTIO

Why, is not this better now than groaning
for love? Now art thou sociable, now art thou
Romeo, now art thou what thou art, by art as well as
by nature. For this driveling love is like a great
natural that runs lolling up and down to hide his
bauble in a hole.

BENVOLIO

Stop there, stop there.

MERCUTIO

Thou desirest me to stop in my tale against
the hair.

BENVOLIO

Thou wouldst else have made thy tale large.

MERCUTIO

O, thou art deceived. I would have made it
short, for I was come to the whole depth of my tale
and meant indeed to occupy the argument no
longer.

Enter Nurse and her man ⸢Peter.⸣

ROMEO

Here’s goodly gear. A sail, a sail!

MERCUTIO

Two, two—a shirt and a smock.

NURSE

Peter.

PETER

Anon.

NURSE

My fan, Peter.

MERCUTIO

Good Peter, to hide her face, for her fan’s
the fairer face.

NURSE

God you good morrow, gentlemen.

MERCUTIO

God you good e’en, fair gentlewoman.

NURSE

Is it good e’en?

MERCUTIO

’Tis no less, I tell you, for the bawdy hand of
the dial is now upon the prick of noon.

NURSE

Out upon you! What a man are you?

ROMEO

One, gentlewoman, that God hath made, himself
to mar.

NURSE

By my troth, it is well said: "for himself to
mar," quoth he? Gentlemen, can any of you tell me
where I may find the young Romeo?

ROMEO

I can tell you, but young Romeo will be older
when you have found him than he was when you
sought him. I am the youngest of that name, for
fault of a worse.

NURSE

You say well.

MERCUTIO

Yea, is the worst well? Very well took, i’
faith, wisely, wisely.

NURSE

If you be he, sir, I desire some confidence with
you.

BENVOLIO

She will indite him to some supper.

MERCUTIO

A bawd, a bawd, a bawd. So ho!

ROMEO

What hast thou found?

MERCUTIO

No hare, sir, unless a hare, sir, in a Lenten
pie that is something stale and hoar ere it be spent.

⸢Singing.⸣

952An old hare hoar,
953And an old hare hoar,
954Is very good meat in Lent.
955But a hare that is hoar
956Is too much for a score
957When it hoars ere it be spent.

Romeo, will you come to your father’s? We’ll to
dinner thither.

ROMEO

I will follow you.

MERCUTIO

Farewell, ancient lady. Farewell, lady, lady,
lady.

⸢Mercutio and Benvolio⸣ exit.

NURSE

I pray you, sir, what saucy merchant was this
that was so full of his ropery?

ROMEO

A gentleman, nurse, that loves to hear himself
talk and will speak more in a minute than he will
stand to in a month.

NURSE

An he speak anything against me, I’ll take him
down, an he were lustier than he is, and twenty
such jacks. An if I cannot, I’ll find those that shall.
Scurvy knave, I am none of his flirt-gills; I am none
of his skains-mates. ⸢To Peter⸣. And thou must stand
by too and suffer every knave to use me at his
pleasure.

PETER

I saw no man use you at his pleasure. If I had,
my weapon should quickly have been out. I warrant
you, I dare draw as soon as another man, if I
see occasion in a good quarrel, and the law on my
side.

NURSE

Now, afore God, I am so vexed that every part
about me quivers. Scurvy knave! ⸢To Romeo⸣. Pray
you, sir, a word. And, as I told you, my young lady
bid me inquire you out. What she bid me say, I will
keep to myself. But first let me tell you, if you
should lead her in a fool’s paradise, as they say, it
were a very gross kind of behavior, as they say. For
the gentlewoman is young; and therefore, if you
should deal double with her, truly it were an ill
thing to be offered to any gentlewoman, and very
weak dealing.

ROMEO

Nurse, commend me to thy lady and mistress.
I protest unto thee—

NURSE

Good heart, and i’ faith I will tell her as much.
Lord, Lord, she will be a joyful woman.

ROMEO

What wilt thou tell her, nurse? Thou dost not
mark me.

NURSE

I will tell her, sir, that you do protest, which, as
I take it, is a gentlemanlike offer.

ROMEO

958Bid her devise
959Some means to come to shrift this afternoon,
960And there she shall at Friar Lawrence’ cell
961Be shrived and married. Here is for thy pains.

⸢Offering her money.⸣

NURSE

No, truly, sir, not a penny.

ROMEO

Go to, I say you shall.

NURSE

962This afternoon, sir? Well, she shall be there.

ROMEO

963And stay, good nurse, behind the abbey wall.
964Within this hour my man shall be with thee
965And bring thee cords made like a tackled stair,
966Which to the high topgallant of my joy
967Must be my convoy in the secret night.
968Farewell. Be trusty, and I’ll quit thy pains.
969Farewell. Commend me to thy mistress.

NURSE

970Now, God in heaven bless thee! Hark you, sir.

ROMEO

971What sayst thou, my dear nurse?

NURSE

972Is your man secret? Did you ne’er hear say
973"Two may keep counsel, putting one away"?

ROMEO

974Warrant thee, my man’s as true as steel.

NURSE

Well, sir, my mistress is the sweetest lady. Lord,
Lord, when ’twas a little prating thing—O, there is
a nobleman in town, one Paris, that would fain lay
knife aboard, but she, good soul, had as lief see a
toad, a very toad, as see him. I anger her sometimes
and tell her that Paris is the properer man, but I’ll
warrant you, when I say so, she looks as pale as any
clout in the versal world. Doth not rosemary and
Romeo begin both with a letter?

ROMEO

Ay, nurse, what of that? Both with an R.

NURSE

Ah, mocker, that’s the ⸢dog’s⸣ name. R is for
the—No, I know it begins with some other letter,
and she hath the prettiest sententious of it, of you
and rosemary, that it would do you good to hear it.

ROMEO

Commend me to thy lady.

NURSE

Ay, a thousand times.—Peter.

PETER

Anon.

NURSE

Before and apace.

⸢They⸣ exit.

⸢Scene 5⸣

Enter Juliet.

JULIET

975The clock struck nine when I did send the Nurse.
976In half an hour she promised to return.
977Perchance she cannot meet him. That’s not so.
978O, she is lame! Love’s heralds should be thoughts,
979Which ten times faster glides than the sun’s beams,
980Driving back shadows over louring hills.
981Therefore do nimble-pinioned doves draw Love,
982And therefore hath the wind-swift Cupid wings.
983Now is the sun upon the highmost hill
984Of this day’s journey, and from nine till twelve
985Is ⸢three⸣ long hours, yet she is not come.
986Had she affections and warm youthful blood,
987She would be as swift in motion as a ball;
988My words would bandy her to my sweet love,
989And his to me.
990But old folks, many feign as they were dead,
991Unwieldy, slow, heavy, and pale as lead.

Enter Nurse ⸢and Peter.⸣

992O God, she comes!—O, honey nurse, what news?
993Hast thou met with him? Send thy man away.

NURSE

Peter, stay at the gate.

⸢Peter exits⸣.

JULIET

994Now, good sweet nurse—O Lord, why lookest thou sad?
995Though news be sad, yet tell them merrily.
996If good, thou shamest the music of sweet news
997By playing it to me with so sour a face.

NURSE

998I am aweary. Give me leave awhile.
999Fie, how my bones ache! What a jaunt have I!

JULIET

1000I would thou hadst my bones, and I thy news.
1001Nay, come, I pray thee, speak. Good, good nurse, speak.

NURSE

1002Jesu, what haste! Can you not stay awhile?
1003Do you not see that I am out of breath?

JULIET

1004How art thou out of breath, when thou hast breath
1005To say to me that thou art out of breath?
1006The excuse that thou dost make in this delay
1007Is longer than the tale thou dost excuse.
1008Is thy news good or bad? Answer to that.
1009Say either, and I’ll stay the circumstance.
1010Let me be satisfied; is ’t good or bad?

NURSE

Well, you have made a simple choice. You know
not how to choose a man. Romeo? No, not he.
Though his face be better than any man’s, yet his leg
excels all men’s, and for a hand and a foot and a
body, though they be not to be talked on, yet they
are past compare. He is not the flower of courtesy,
but I’ll warrant him as gentle as a lamb. Go thy
ways, wench. Serve God. What, have you dined at
home?

JULIET

1011No, no. But all this did I know before.
1012What says he of our marriage? What of that?

NURSE

1013Lord, how my head aches! What a head have I!
1014It beats as it would fall in twenty pieces.
1015My back o’ t’ other side! Ah, my back, my back!
1016Beshrew your heart for sending me about
1017To catch my death with jaunting up and down.

JULIET

1018I’ faith, I am sorry that thou art not well.
1019Sweet, sweet, sweet nurse, tell me, what says my love?

NURSE

Your love says, like an honest gentleman, and a
courteous, and a kind, and a handsome, and, I
warrant, a virtuous—Where is your mother?

JULIET

1020Where is my mother? Why, she is within.
1021Where should she be? How oddly thou repliest:
1022"Your love says, like an honest gentleman,
1023Where is your mother?"

NURSE

Where is your mother?" O God’s lady dear,
1024Are you so hot? Marry, come up, I trow.
1025Is this the poultice for my aching bones?
1026Henceforward do your messages yourself.

JULIET

1027Here’s such a coil. Come, what says Romeo?

NURSE

1028Have you got leave to go to shrift today?

JULIET

I have.

NURSE

1029Then hie you hence to Friar Lawrence’ cell.
1030There stays a husband to make you a wife.
1031Now comes the wanton blood up in your cheeks;
1032They’ll be in scarlet straight at any news.
1033Hie you to church. I must another way,
1034To fetch a ladder by the which your love
1035Must climb a bird’s nest soon when it is dark.
1036I am the drudge and toil in your delight,
1037But you shall bear the burden soon at night.
1038Go. I’ll to dinner. Hie you to the cell.

JULIET

1039Hie to high fortune! Honest nurse, farewell.

They exit.

⸢Scene 6⸣

Enter Friar ⸢Lawrence⸣ and Romeo.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1040So smile the heavens upon this holy act
1041That after-hours with sorrow chide us not.

ROMEO

1042Amen, amen. But come what sorrow can,
1043It cannot countervail the exchange of joy
1044That one short minute gives me in her sight.
1045Do thou but close our hands with holy words,
1046Then love-devouring death do what he dare,
1047It is enough I may but call her mine.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1048These violent delights have violent ends
1049And in their triumph die, like fire and powder,
1050Which, as they kiss, consume. The sweetest honey
1051Is loathsome in his own deliciousness
1052And in the taste confounds the appetite.
1053Therefore love moderately. Long love doth so.
1054Too swift arrives as tardy as too slow.

Enter Juliet.

1055Here comes the lady. O, so light a foot
1056Will ne’er wear out the everlasting flint.
1057A lover may bestride the gossamers
1058That idles in the wanton summer air,
1059And yet not fall, so light is vanity.

JULIET

1060Good even to my ghostly confessor.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1061Romeo shall thank thee, daughter, for us both.

JULIET

1062As much to him, else is his thanks too much.

ROMEO

1063Ah, Juliet, if the measure of thy joy
1064Be heaped like mine, and that thy skill be more
1065To blazon it, then sweeten with thy breath
1066This neighbor air, and let rich ⸢music’s⸣ tongue
1067Unfold the imagined happiness that both
1068Receive in either by this dear encounter.

JULIET

1069Conceit, more rich in matter than in words,
1070Brags of his substance, not of ornament.
1071They are but beggars that can count their worth,
1072But my true love is grown to such excess
1073I cannot sum up sum of half my wealth.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1074Come, come with me, and we will make short work,
1075For, by your leaves, you shall not stay alone
1076Till Holy Church incorporate two in one.

⸢They exit.⸣

Act III

⸢Scene 1⸣

Enter Mercutio, Benvolio, and ⸢their⸣ men.

BENVOLIO

1077I pray thee, good Mercutio, let’s retire.
1078The day is hot, the Capels ⸢are⸣ abroad,
1079And if we meet we shall not ’scape a brawl,
1080For now, these hot days, is the mad blood stirring.

MERCUTIO

Thou art like one of these fellows that, when
he enters the confines of a tavern, claps me his
sword upon the table and says "God send me no
need of thee" and, by the operation of the second
cup, draws him on the drawer when indeed there is
no need.

BENVOLIO

Am I like such a fellow?

MERCUTIO

Come, come, thou art as hot a jack in thy
mood as any in Italy, and as soon moved to be
moody, and as soon moody to be moved.

BENVOLIO

And what to?

MERCUTIO

Nay, an there were two such, we should
have none shortly, for one would kill the other.
Thou—why, thou wilt quarrel with a man that
hath a hair more or a hair less in his beard than
thou hast. Thou wilt quarrel with a man for cracking
nuts, having no other reason but because thou
hast hazel eyes. What eye but such an eye would spy
out such a quarrel? Thy head is as full of quarrels as
an egg is full of meat, and yet thy head hath been
beaten as addle as an egg for quarreling. Thou hast
quarreled with a man for coughing in the street
because he hath wakened thy dog that hath lain
asleep in the sun. Didst thou not fall out with a tailor
for wearing his new doublet before Easter? With
another, for tying his new shoes with old ribbon?
And yet thou wilt tutor me from quarreling?

BENVOLIO

An I were so apt to quarrel as thou art, any
man should buy the fee simple of my life for an
hour and a quarter.

MERCUTIO

The fee simple? O simple!

Enter Tybalt, Petruchio, and others.

BENVOLIO

By my head, here comes the Capulets.

MERCUTIO

By my heel, I care not.

TYBALT

1081, ⸢to his companions⸣ Follow me close, for I will speak to them.—
1082Gentlemen, good e’en. A word with one of you.

MERCUTIO

And but one word with one of us? Couple it
with something. Make it a word and a blow.

TYBALT

You shall find me apt enough to that, sir, an
you will give me occasion.

MERCUTIO

Could you not take some occasion without
giving?

TYBALT

Mercutio, thou consortest with Romeo.

MERCUTIO

Consort? What, dost thou make us minstrels?
An thou make minstrels of us, look to hear
nothing but discords. Here’s my fiddlestick; here’s
that shall make you dance. Zounds, consort!

BENVOLIO

1083We talk here in the public haunt of men.
1084Either withdraw unto some private place,
1085Or reason coldly of your grievances,
1086Or else depart. Here all eyes gaze on us.

MERCUTIO

1087Men’s eyes were made to look, and let them gaze.
1088I will not budge for no man’s pleasure, I.

Enter Romeo.

TYBALT

1089Well, peace be with you, sir. Here comes my man.

MERCUTIO

1090But I’ll be hanged, sir, if he wear your livery.
1091Marry, go before to field, he’ll be your follower.
1092Your Worship in that sense may call him "man."

TYBALT

1093Romeo, the love I bear thee can afford
1094No better term than this: thou art a villain.

ROMEO

1095Tybalt, the reason that I have to love thee
1096Doth much excuse the appertaining rage
1097To such a greeting. Villain am I none.
1098Therefore farewell. I see thou knowest me not.

TYBALT

1099Boy, this shall not excuse the injuries
1100That thou hast done me. Therefore turn and draw.

ROMEO

1101I do protest I never injured thee
1102But love thee better than thou canst devise
1103Till thou shalt know the reason of my love.
1104And so, good Capulet, which name I tender
1105As dearly as mine own, be satisfied.

MERCUTIO

1106O calm, dishonorable, vile submission!
1107Allas toccato carries it away.

⸢He draws.⸣

1108Tybalt, you ratcatcher, will you walk?

TYBALT

What wouldst thou have with me?

MERCUTIO

Good king of cats, nothing but one of your
nine lives, that I mean to make bold withal, and, as
you shall use me hereafter, dry-beat the rest of the
eight. Will you pluck your sword out of his pilcher
by the ears? Make haste, lest mine be about your
ears ere it be out.

TYBALT

I am for you.

⸢He draws.⸣

ROMEO

1109Gentle Mercutio, put thy rapier up.

MERCUTIO

1110Come, sir, your passado.

⸢They fight.⸣

ROMEO

1111Draw, Benvolio, beat down their weapons.

⸢Romeo draws.⸣

1112Gentlemen, for shame forbear this outrage!
1113Tybalt! Mercutio! The Prince expressly hath
1114Forbid this bandying in Verona streets.
1115Hold, Tybalt! Good Mercutio!

⸢Romeo attempts to beat down their rapiers⸣. ⸢Tybalt stabs Mercutio⸣.

⸢PETRUCHIO⸣

Away, Tybalt!

⸢Tybalt, Petruchio, and their followers exit.⸣

MERCUTIO

1116I am hurt.
1117A plague o’ both houses! I am sped.
1118Is he gone and hath nothing?

BENVOLIO

Is he gone and hath nothing? What, art thou hurt?

MERCUTIO

1119Ay, ay, a scratch, a scratch. Marry, ’tis enough.
1120Where is my page?—Go, villain, fetch a surgeon.

⸢Page exits.⸣

ROMEO

1121Courage, man, the hurt cannot be much.

MERCUTIO

No, ’tis not so deep as a well, nor so wide as
a church door, but ’tis enough. ’Twill serve. Ask for
me tomorrow, and you shall find me a grave man. I
am peppered, I warrant, for this world. A plague o’
both your houses! Zounds, a dog, a rat, a mouse, a
cat, to scratch a man to death! A braggart, a rogue, a
villain that fights by the book of arithmetic! Why the
devil came you between us? I was hurt under your
arm.

ROMEO

1122I thought all for the best.

MERCUTIO

1123Help me into some house, Benvolio,
1124Or I shall faint. A plague o’ both your houses!
1125They have made worms’ meat of me.
1126I have it, and soundly, too. Your houses!

⸢All but Romeo⸣ exit.

ROMEO

1127This gentleman, the Prince’s near ally,
1128My very friend, hath got this mortal hurt
1129In my behalf. My reputation stained
1130With Tybalt’s slander—Tybalt, that an hour
1131Hath been my cousin! O sweet Juliet,
1132Thy beauty hath made me effeminate
1133And in my temper softened valor’s steel.

Enter Benvolio.

BENVOLIO

1134O Romeo, Romeo, brave Mercutio is dead.
1135That gallant spirit hath aspired the clouds,
1136Which too untimely here did scorn the earth.

ROMEO

1137This day’s black fate on more days doth depend.
1138This but begins the woe others must end.

⸢Enter Tybalt.⸣

BENVOLIO

1139Here comes the furious Tybalt back again.

ROMEO

1140⸢Alive⸣ in triumph, and Mercutio slain!
1141Away to heaven, respective lenity,
1142And ⸢fire-eyed⸣ fury be my conduct now.—
1143Now, Tybalt, take the "villain" back again
1144That late thou gavest me, for Mercutio’s soul
1145Is but a little way above our heads,
1146Staying for thine to keep him company.
1147Either thou or I, or both, must go with him.

TYBALT

1148Thou wretched boy that didst consort him here
1149Shalt with him hence.

ROMEO

Shalt with him hence. This shall determine that.

They fight. Tybalt falls.

BENVOLIO

1150Romeo, away, begone!
1151The citizens are up, and Tybalt slain.
1152Stand not amazed. The Prince will doom thee death
1153If thou art taken. Hence, be gone, away.

ROMEO

1154O, I am Fortune’s fool!

BENVOLIO

O, I am Fortune’s fool! Why dost thou stay?

Romeo exits.

Enter Citizens.

CITIZEN

1155Which way ran he that killed Mercutio?
1156Tybalt, that murderer, which way ran he?

BENVOLIO

1157There lies that Tybalt.

CITIZEN

There lies that Tybalt. , ⸢to Tybalt⸣ Up, sir, go with me.
1158I charge thee in the Prince’s name, obey.

Enter Prince, old Montague, Capulet, their Wives and all.

PRINCE

1159Where are the vile beginners of this fray?

BENVOLIO

1160O noble prince, I can discover all
1161The unlucky manage of this fatal brawl.
1162There lies the man, slain by young Romeo,
1163That slew thy kinsman, brave Mercutio.

LADY CAPULET

1164Tybalt, my cousin, O my brother’s child!
1165O prince! O cousin! Husband! O, the blood is spilled
1166Of my dear kinsman! Prince, as thou art true,
1167For blood of ours, shed blood of Montague.
1168O cousin, cousin!

PRINCE

1169Benvolio, who began this bloody fray?

BENVOLIO

1170Tybalt, here slain, whom Romeo’s hand did slay—
1171Romeo, that spoke him fair, bid him bethink
1172How nice the quarrel was, and urged withal
1173Your high displeasure. All this utterèd
1174With gentle breath, calm look, knees humbly bowed
1175Could not take truce with the unruly spleen
1176Of Tybalt, deaf to peace, but that he tilts
1177With piercing steel at bold Mercutio’s breast,
1178Who, all as hot, turns deadly point to point
1179And, with a martial scorn, with one hand beats
1180Cold death aside and with the other sends
1181It back to Tybalt, whose dexterity
1182Retorts it. Romeo he cries aloud
1183"Hold, friends! Friends, part!" and swifter than his tongue
1184His ⸢agile⸣ arm beats down their fatal points,
1185And ’twixt them rushes; underneath whose arm
1186An envious thrust from Tybalt hit the life
1187Of stout Mercutio, and then Tybalt fled.
1188But by and by comes back to Romeo,
1189Who had but newly entertained revenge,
1190And to ’t they go like lightning, for ere I
1191Could draw to part them was stout Tybalt slain,
1192And, as he fell, did Romeo turn and fly.
1193This is the truth, or let Benvolio die.

LADY CAPULET

1194He is a kinsman to the Montague.
1195Affection makes him false; he speaks not true.
1196Some twenty of them fought in this black strife,
1197And all those twenty could but kill one life.
1198I beg for justice, which thou, prince, must give.
1199Romeo slew Tybalt; Romeo must not live.

PRINCE

1200Romeo slew him; he slew Mercutio.
1201Who now the price of his dear blood doth owe?

⸢MONTAGUE⸣

1202Not Romeo, Prince; he was Mercutio’s friend.
1203His fault concludes but what the law should end,
1204The life of Tybalt.

PRINCE

The life of Tybalt. And for that offense
1205Immediately we do exile him hence.
1206I have an interest in your hearts’ proceeding:
1207My blood for your rude brawls doth lie a-bleeding.
1208But I’ll amerce you with so strong a fine
1209That you shall all repent the loss of mine.
1210⸢I⸣ will be deaf to pleading and excuses.
1211Nor tears nor prayers shall purchase out abuses.
1212Therefore use none. Let Romeo hence in haste,
1213Else, when he is found, that hour is his last.
1214Bear hence this body and attend our will.
1215Mercy but murders, pardoning those that kill.

⸢They⸣ exit, ⸢the Capulet men bearing off Tybalt’s body.⸣

⸢Scene 2⸣

Enter Juliet alone.

JULIET

1216Gallop apace, you fiery-footed steeds,
1217Towards Phoebus’ lodging. Such a wagoner
1218As Phaëton would whip you to the west
1219And bring in cloudy night immediately.
1220Spread thy close curtain, love-performing night,
1221That runaways’ eyes may wink, and Romeo
1222Leap to these arms, untalked of and unseen.
1223Lovers can see to do their amorous rites
1224By their own beauties, or, if love be blind,
1225It best agrees with night. Come, civil night,
1226Thou sober-suited matron all in black,
1227And learn me how to lose a winning match
1228Played for a pair of stainless maidenhoods.
1229Hood my unmanned blood, bating in my cheeks,
1230With thy black mantle till strange love grow bold,
1231Think true love acted simple modesty.
1232Come, night. Come, Romeo. Come, thou day in night,
1233For thou wilt lie upon the wings of night
1234Whiter than new snow upon a raven’s back.
1235Come, gentle night; come, loving black-browed night,
1236Give me my Romeo, and when I shall die,
1237Take him and cut him out in little stars,
1238And he will make the face of heaven so fine
1239That all the world will be in love with night
1240And pay no worship to the garish sun.
1241O, I have bought the mansion of a love
1242But not possessed it, and, though I am sold,
1243Not yet enjoyed. So tedious is this day
1244As is the night before some festival
1245To an impatient child that hath new robes
1246And may not wear them.

Enter Nurse with cords.

And may not wear them. O, here comes my nurse,
1247And she brings news, and every tongue that speaks
1248But Romeo’s name speaks heavenly eloquence.—
1249Now, nurse, what news? What hast thou there? The cords
1250That Romeo bid thee fetch?

NURSE

That Romeo bid thee fetch? Ay, ay, the cords.

⸢Dropping the rope ladder.⸣

JULIET

1251Ay me, what news? Why dost thou wring thy hands?

NURSE

1252Ah weraday, he’s dead, he’s dead, he’s dead!
1253We are undone, lady, we are undone.
1254Alack the day, he’s gone, he’s killed, he’s dead.

JULIET

1255Can heaven be so envious?

NURSE

Can heaven be so envious? Romeo can,
1256Though heaven cannot. O Romeo, Romeo,
1257Whoever would have thought it? Romeo!

JULIET

1258What devil art thou that dost torment me thus?
1259This torture should be roared in dismal hell.
1260Hath Romeo slain himself? Say thou but "Ay,"
1261And that bare vowel "I" shall poison more
1262Than the death-darting eye of cockatrice.
1263I am not I if there be such an "I,"
1264Or those eyes ⸢shut⸣ that makes thee answer "Ay."
1265If he be slain, say "Ay," or if not, "No."
1266Brief sounds determine my weal or woe.

NURSE

1267I saw the wound. I saw it with mine eyes
1268(God save the mark!) here on his manly breast—
1269A piteous corse, a bloody piteous corse,
1270Pale, pale as ashes, all bedaubed in blood,
1271All in gore blood. I swoonèd at the sight.

JULIET

1272O break, my heart, poor bankrout, break at once!
1273To prison, eyes; ne’er look on liberty.
1274Vile earth to earth resign; end motion here,
1275And thou and Romeo press one heavy bier.

NURSE

1276O Tybalt, Tybalt, the best friend I had!
1277O courteous Tybalt, honest gentleman,
1278That ever I should live to see thee dead!

JULIET

1279What storm is this that blows so contrary?
1280Is Romeo slaughtered and is Tybalt dead?
1281My dearest cousin, and my dearer lord?
1282Then, dreadful trumpet, sound the general doom,
1283For who is living if those two are gone?

NURSE

1284Tybalt is gone and Romeo banishèd.
1285Romeo that killed him—he is banishèd.

JULIET

1286O God, did Romeo’s hand shed Tybalt’s blood?

⸢NURSE⸣

1287It did, it did, alas the day, it did.

⸢JULIET⸣

1288O serpent heart hid with a flow’ring face!
1289Did ever dragon keep so fair a cave?
1290Beautiful tyrant, fiend angelical!
1291Dove-feathered raven, wolvish-ravening lamb!
1292Despisèd substance of divinest show!
1293Just opposite to what thou justly seem’st,
1294A ⸢damnèd⸣ saint, an honorable villain.
1295O nature, what hadst thou to do in hell
1296When thou didst bower the spirit of a fiend
1297In mortal paradise of such sweet flesh?
1298Was ever book containing such vile matter
1299So fairly bound? O, that deceit should dwell
1300In such a gorgeous palace!

NURSE

In such a gorgeous palace! There’s no trust,
1301No faith, no honesty in men. All perjured,
1302All forsworn, all naught, all dissemblers.
1303Ah, where’s my man? Give me some aqua vitae.
1304These griefs, these woes, these sorrows make me old.
1305Shame come to Romeo!

JULIET

Shame come to Romeo! Blistered be thy tongue
1306For such a wish! He was not born to shame.
1307Upon his brow shame is ashamed to sit,
1308For ’tis a throne where honor may be crowned
1309Sole monarch of the universal Earth.
1310O, what a beast was I to chide at him!

NURSE

1311Will you speak well of him that killed your cousin?

JULIET

1312Shall I speak ill of him that is my husband?
1313Ah, poor my lord, what tongue shall smooth thy name
1314When I, thy three-hours wife, have mangled it?
1315But wherefore, villain, didst thou kill my cousin?
1316That villain cousin would have killed my husband.
1317Back, foolish tears, back to your native spring;
1318Your tributary drops belong to woe,
1319Which you, mistaking, offer up to joy.
1320My husband lives, that Tybalt would have slain,
1321And Tybalt’s dead, that would have slain my husband.
1322All this is comfort. Wherefore weep I then?
1323Some word there was, worser than Tybalt’s death,
1324That murdered me. I would forget it fain,
1325But, O, it presses to my memory
1326Like damnèd guilty deeds to sinners’ minds:
1327"Tybalt is dead and Romeo banishèd."
1328That "banishèd," that one word "banishèd,"
1329Hath slain ten thousand Tybalts. Tybalt’s death
1330Was woe enough if it had ended there;
1331Or, if sour woe delights in fellowship
1332And needly will be ranked with other griefs,
1333Why followed not, when she said "Tybalt’s dead,"
1334"Thy father" or "thy mother," nay, or both,
1335Which modern lamentation might have moved?
1336But with a rearward following Tybalt’s death,
1337"Romeo is banishèd." To speak that word
1338Is father, mother, Tybalt, Romeo, Juliet,
1339All slain, all dead. "Romeo is banishèd."
1340There is no end, no limit, measure, bound,
1341In that word’s death. No words can that woe sound.
1342Where is my father and my mother, nurse?

NURSE

1343Weeping and wailing over Tybalt’s corse.
1344Will you go to them? I will bring you thither.

JULIET

1345Wash they his wounds with tears? Mine shall be spent,
1346When theirs are dry, for Romeo’s banishment.—
1347Take up those cords.

⸢The Nurse picks up the rope ladder.⸣

Take up those cords. Poor ropes, you are beguiled,
1348Both you and I, for Romeo is exiled.
1349He made you for a highway to my bed,
1350But I, a maid, die maiden-widowèd.
1351Come, cords—come, nurse. I’ll to my wedding bed,
1352And death, not Romeo, take my maidenhead!

NURSE

1353Hie to your chamber. I’ll find Romeo
1354To comfort you. I wot well where he is.
1355Hark you, your Romeo will be here at night.
1356I’ll to him. He is hid at Lawrence’ cell.

JULIET

1357O, find him!
